497. MARY ANNE SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of January, 4 handkerchiefs, value 16s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 6s., and 12 yards of ribbon, value 16s., the goods of Samuel Strong and Richard Woolley.
CHARLES SCOTT. I am in the service of Messrs. Strong and Woolley - they are silk-mercer s. On the 3rd of January, about noon, the prisoner came to their shop - she purchased a number of articles, which came to 4l. 13s.; she desired them to be sent to a direction which she gave; I mentioned it to Mr. Woolley, who followed her.
RICHARD WOOLLEY. I am in partnership with Mr. Samuel Strong. On the 3rd of January I followed the prisoner to her lodgings, in Peter-street, Cow-cross - I first saw her on the stairs; I got her to come down, and sent the boy for an officer - she denied any knowledge of me, but I told her she had been in my shop a short time before, and if she would give up what property she had of mine, I would suspend the charge, if the constable would; she then took me up stairs to her room, and gave me two silk handkerchiefs out of her bed, which was turned up; I said she must have some ribbon - she then produced this ribbon; the officer then came, and he found these other handkerchiefs and stockings in the bed.(Property produced and sworn to.)
RICHARD MILLEN. I am an officer. I found these handkerchiefs and stockings doubled up in the bed; this ribbon and these other handkerchiefs the prosecutor had in his hand.
JURY. Q. Did any one else occupy that room? A. The person who let the room said it was let to the prisoner only.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, pleading poverty, and expressing her contrition.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 1 Month.
[trialAccount t18320105-205]
498. JOHN SIBLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of October, 1 goose, value 4s., the property of Thomas Payne.
SARAH PAYNE. I am the wife of Thomas Payne. On the night of the 22nd of October, we lost three geese, one was brought back by the officer - I knew it was one we had lost.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long was that after you missed it? A. I saw it safe on the Saturday night, and the officer showed it me again on the Sunday night; it was then dead; it was alive when I saw it on the Saturday - the head and wing of it are here.
COURT. Q. Was the place in which the geese were, broken? A. Yes, the staple was drawn.
MARK WALSH. I am an officer of South-mimms. I received information, and went to Mr. Bull's, who keeps an eating-house at Hadley, on the 23rd of October, about half-past six o'clock in the evening, I found a goose dead, but quite warm - I showed it to Mr. Pitman, and then Mr. Paine; they claimed it: there were some marks of blood on the back of the neck, but none on the prisoner when I took him.
JOSEPH BULL. I live at Hadley. On the 23rd of October the prisoner came to my house, about half-past one o'clock; he asked what I had done with my ducks - I said I had got rid of some, and some persons had got rid of some for me; he then asked how geese were - I said they were too dear for me; he said he knew where there were some to be had cheap - I said I supposed on the cross - he said No, it was all right, that he had them from a person he had been ditching for; I said I had no goose money - he paid for what he had, and went away: he came again in about an hour, with a goose in a sack, and asked if he could leave it there, and call for the sack by and by - he gave it to my wife, and as he passed me he said, "The goose is in that;" there was one in it, which did not appear to have been killed above ten or twelve minutes - the head was nearly severed from the body; I put it into thesack again - I afterwards delivered the same geese to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. Had the sack been out of your sight? A. It had not been out of my premises; he was not in the habit of selling geese; I meant by the cross, that he had come by it in a dishonest manner; I said I would have nothing to do with it, but he brought it.
SARAH PAYNE. This is the head and wing of the goose.
COURT. Q. Had you fed and observed your geese? A. Yes; I had had them about three months - I can say, from my observation of it, this was one of mine; Southmimms is about fourteen miles from town, and Mr. Bull's is about three miles from me.
GUILTY. Aged 60. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18320105-206]

500. JOHN RICHARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of November, 12 silver spoons, value 3l.; 3 pairs of window-curtains, value 3l.; 12 brass books, value 5s., and 20 yards of braiding, value 5s., the goods of Charles Wardsworth, in his dwelling-house.
MR. BARRY conducted the prosecution.
JAMES AYLETT. I am a gentleman's servant. In October and November I was in the service of Mr. Wardsworth; I had only been in his service a few days - I was at his dwelling-house, No. 3, Clifford-street, Bond-street; I recollect the curtains in the front dining-room - they were red silk: they had been put up before I came into his service, which was five days or a week before this matter happened; I saw some books and rings - there was some braiding round the curtains, and there was a dozen silver spoons: I saw them about a quarter of an hour before this happened - they laid on a sideboard by the side of the fire-place. On the 18th of November I heard a knock at the door; I had been in the dining-room about a quarter of an hour before them, locked the door, and put the key under the mat, close by the door - it was then I saw the silver spoons; a woman, who was housekeeper, was in the house with me; on hearing the knock I opened the door, and saw the prisoner and Willis, who rushed in as soon as I opened it - Willis said, "I want to see Mr. Wardsworth;" I said he was not at home - before that he turned the handle of the parlour door, which was locked, and then ran up to the drawing-room; I followed him - he looked over the rooms, to satisfy himself that Mr. Wardsworth was not there - he looked over different rooms; he was up stairs four or five minutes; I then followed him down stairs, and saw the dining-room door open; Willis could not have opened it, because he was with me - nobody could have opened it but the prisoner - I found the prisoner in the dining-room alone, taking down the curtains; I asked what he wanted - he said, "My name is John Richards - these are mine;" Pattempted to lock him into the room - he took down a wire blind, opened the window, and was going to give them to a person who was waiting in the street; I have not seen that person since -I did not lock the door, and he gave them out to the person at the front door - they were all taken away; the spoons were also gone - I am sure the spoons were in the room ten minutes or a quarter of an hour before; Willis said to the prisoner, "What, have you taken them? mind, I have no hand in this."
Cross-examined by MR. ALLEY. Q. You had no previous acquaintance with the prosecutor? A. I had known him some little time before - I knew him when he was in the Marshalsea twelve months ago; I was not a creditor of his - I do not know the amount of the debt he was confined for - Clifford-street is a fashionable street; it is at the corner of Burlington-street; I was not acting as footman; I said I was his servant - I was out of livery; I was there about a week.
Q. You are not a broker who takes possession for other people? A. I have been in possession once for the prosecutor; I have been in a house with a bill of sale - I was in this house afterwards with a bill of sale, in possession for a person named Cooper; I went into possession on the evening of the 18th - the deed was executed that day.
Q. Then you having been servant to the prosecutor five days, on the fifth day take a bill of sale from Cooper to take your master's goods? A. I took a bill of sale - I was merely attending for Mr. Wardsworth while he got a little money advanced on the goods; I then became servant to Mr. Cooper - he is a wharfinger, and lives at Pickle Herring-wharf; I believe he has the bill of sale - the goods were all taken away; not under the bill of sale about a dozen men came in one day, and held me down while they took the goods away - I have been a brewer, and have very likely said that I was a broken down brewer; I was put in possession of the bill of sale about five or six o'clock, the same night the curtains were taken away, which was between eleven and twelve o'clock; my master had come home about five; I was put in possession - I had seen Cooper that day; I have seen him and my master visit, perhaps five or six times, about a year and a half ago - I do not know where the woman remained while I and Willis went up stairs; I believe Willis is a bailiff, and had come to arrest my master - the prisoner had been to the house the day before I believe; the goods were taken in open day.
Q. Now, be cautious, did you not say you were waiting in the house, and would not say you was in possession till more goods came in? A. No; nor that I would remain in the house as the prosecutor's servant, and not take possession till more goods came in, for it was not worth while - nor to that effect; I did not say I was waiting to take possession when more goods came in, nor any thing to that effect.
Q. When you went up stairs with the bailiff, was not the maid servant in the hall? A. No, I believe not - she is not here; I do not know where she is - I know I left her down stairs; there was no other servant in the house; I do not know Mr. Wilkinson, a pianoforte-maker - I recollect a person taking a pianoforte away; that might be a fortnight ago - no complaint was made against him, to my knowledge: the spoons which were taken were tea-spoons - I do not know where they were bought; I never heard master say he had a large quantity of plate coming home, with a crest on it; these had no crest - they were nearly new - I did not know Mr. Richards, the linen-draper; the bailiff did not say he had come to arrest master - he said he had a writ against him; the prisoner said he was Mr. Richards, and took away the curtains - I never went to Mr. Richards to claim the property back, nor did Mr. Wardsworth, to my knowledge.
Q. The spoons were never claimed by any body, only the curtains - then the spoons, at all events, must have been stolen? A. Certainly; I went before the Magistrate after I had a true bill found against him.
COURT. Q. A true bill could not be found till the Grand Jury sat - did you complain to any Magistrate? A. Not to my knowledge.
MR. ALLEY. The Sessions, if I remember, were on the 4th or 5th of December, at Clerknewell? A. It was at Westminster - my first appearance as an accuser was before the Grand Jury; I dare say that was about a fortnight after the goods were taken - I believe the prisoner was taken up the first day of the last Session; the prosecutor went to Mr. Clark's office, got a certificate of the indictment, and then went to the Magistrate - they took him that evening on the certificate, in Mr. Richard's shop.
Q. Did you take him before a Magistrate? A. No, he was taken to the Police-station, and then discharged; he was taken before Mr. Minshull about a fortnight or three weeks before Christmas - my master did not attend then to make a charge against him; I went there - I got there about half-past twelve o'clock; he was then liberated on bail - I went to inquire, and found him liberated; I did not go before the Magistrate.
Q. Now, look at this gentleman, (Mr. Harmer) - on your oath, do you mean to say you went at twelve or half-past twelve o'clock, and the prisoner was discharged? A. Half-past twelve o'clock; I went to inquire of Mr. Burchall - I went into the clerk's office, and inquired there.
Q. Do not you know that Mr. Minshull, hearing the story from Mr. Richard's employer, took his own recognizance to appear? A. I do not know, nor do I know that he has surrendered voluntarily, nor that he has never been in Newgate - I believe it.
COURT. Q. Did you see him as he walked here today? A. No.
MR. ALLEY. Q. Do not you know this trial stood over from morning to morning here for the prisoner to surrender? A. I have heard it; I do not know it - I was here yesterday, but I do not know that he was not tried because he was not in custody; I have not seen him waiting about to surrender - master has not told me that he had not surrendered; I have not received any notice of his intention to surrender, neither last Session nor this - I do not know that he has been on bail ever since; I was told he was on bail.
Q. During the five days you were there, a great many people came after your master? A. Yes, several people who came after bills for goods sold; very little came into the house while I was there - there were a few things, such as eatables and beer; no furniture, nor any thing of that kind.
Q. Am I to understand you to swear that no person came to take away different articles? A. After that they did.
Q. Alleging that they had been swindled out of them? A. Yes.
MR. BARRY. Q. An indictment was found against the prisoner? A. Yes; he was afterwards apprehended on a warrant, a fortnight or three weeks before Christmas.
COURT. Q. Did you know where he lived? A. Yes, because we had apprehended him - I do not know why he was not taken before; I merely went to identify him - I did not make any deposition; I told Mr. Wardsworth what had happened.
CHARLES WARDSWORTH. I am quite out of business; I have been in the Manchester trade. In October and November I occupied the house, No. 3, Clifford-street, Bond-street; I had some dealings with John Richards and Co. of Oxford-street, and purchased the goods named in this bill,(looking at it); I bought some crimson silk curtains - I believe I had six brass hooks of Mr. Richards; the bill amounts to 31l. 19s. 10d. - I had known Mr. Richards about twenty-eight years; he said nothing about the mode of payment - I called on him before I ordered them: he had a very large stock of goods, and was very desirous of selling me goods to furnish my house; he proposed to me to purchase not only these, but other goods - I received the goods named in the invoice, part on the 19th of October, and part on the 28th; I had some servants to make the silk up into window-curtains - part of them were put up in Clifford-street; I used about three dozen hooks - I believe about two dozen were taken away with the curtains - I had purchased part of the braiding of Mr. Richards; that was part of the braiding included in the bill - I also used on the curtains other braiding, and hooks, besides those supplied by him; I was in the house on the morning of the 18th of November, and left between nine and ten o'clock, leaving Aylett behind - I had a woman in the house; I breakfasted in the parlour - the curtains were all up then, and there were a dozen silver spoons, which I intended to take away with me, had I not been obliged to go out on important business, and I left them on a small sideboard; I returned about five o'clock - the curtains and spoons were gone; I was informed a person, describing himself as Richards, had been there - I spoke to my solicitor, and the Sessions were just on; I immediately went to find the bill, the Session being two or three days after; it was the Westminster Sessions - I had a good deal to attend to of importance: I know I went the first day of Westminster Sessions, and found the bill.
Q. What did you do on finding the bill? A. I went to the Clerk of the Peace, in Carlisle-street, Soho, and I was to wait - I was referred to the Old Bailey; I applied here on the first day of the Session - Mr. Clark or his servant told me I must wait till the first day of the Session; I could not have a warrant till then, because the Jury must sit; I obtained the certificate, and got a Police-serjeant and his man that very evening, and took the party to identify him - I took him to the station-house in Marylebone-lane; there he was known to the party, and they discharged him - I was recommended to go to Scotland-yard against the Police; Iwent to Bow-street three or four weeks afterwards, and obtained a warrant, which was executed - I did not attend at Bow-street when he was taken on the warrant, because I did not know of it - I have not got any of my property back.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you sure you should know the property again if you saw it? A. I do not know that I should - I did not put the stitching in it; I did not make them - I was in business last at Manchester at Waterloo-place, Southwark, No. 2; I was in business there from the time of the panic till about two years ago - I was there from 1824 to 1828; I do not recollect what month in 1828 - I had a docket struck against me there, which I superseded; I left Waterloo-place about two days before Christmas - I have done a little commission business since that, in buying and selling malt; I was a spirit-merchant at Manchester - I have not dealt with many persons in the malt line in London.
Q. Were you in any line? A. I have got so many things in Chancery, I have done no business except buying a trifling article or two - I have done nothing but the malt commission business since I came to London.
Q. Where did you live first when you came to London? A. I stopped sometime at a public-house at Islington - I lived there three or four months; it is at the corner of Sun-street, about half a dozen doors from Finsbury-square - I got out of the coach at Islington, and stopped at the Green Man, opposite the Angel, about four days, and then came to lodge at this house; I do not recollect the sign - it is the corner of Sun-street and Crown-street; one end runs to Bishopsgate-street - I do not recollect the landlord's name: I paid him a week in advance for the lodging - I took no receipt, I had witnesses enough - I then took the remaining part of a lease from a person named Benjamin, in Union-court; I believe it was No. 13 - I was there about six months; it was No. 13 or 14, I do not know which - Mr. Stirtevant, the Common Councilman, was my landlord - I went from there to Stingo-lane, No. 8, and have lived there from that time to this, pretty well occasionally.
Q. The inquiry is not where your family lived, but where you yourself lived? A. All my family lived in Union-court; I lived in Stingo-lane two or three months - my family did not live there; I staid in Stingo-lane two or three months.
Q. Where did you go to from Stingo-lane? A. I lived at Sydney-terrace, Sydney-square, No. 2; my family lived there at the same time - I went from there to No. 10, Sutton-street, close by; I stopped there six months - I stopped three months myself, and left my family there; I think I was in Sydney-terrace near six months - I was arrested in Sutton-street, and went to the Marshalsea; I was arrested for three or four sums on some bills - I do not know the amount together; they swore to sums and upwards - I defended the actions myself; I have had a verdict for the third - and the fourth I compromised, to prevent any difficulties.
Q. After defending three actions, what was the sum you remained in prison for? A. Why, near 30l. - I remained there a few weeks for that sum; when one person arrested me, and went to trial - then they lodged a detainer; I was there about a month - I am married: I took two ladies to Mr. Richards - I was there four times, and think I took two; I am not quite positive: I did not take my wife there- it was after I got out of the Marshalsea; I did not tell Mr. Richards I had been in the Marshalsea - what occasion was there for that? he knew me when I had 1000l. in my pocket, and he had not 1s. - I did not promise to pay him ready money for the goods; I bought the spoons in question from Thomas Wilkinson, Piccadilly - here is the bill and receipt: they amount to 4l. 6s.; they were paid for before they were stolen - I never had an office in Somerset-house; I did not represent to Mr. Richards that I had an office in Somerset-house, nor a place, nor any employment there, nothing of the kind; I never said so to the prisoner, or to any body.
Q. Did you not say to him, when he came with 7l. worth of goods, which is the last portion, that you could not pay him till you got your money, but you would pay him when the quarter was up? A. No, nothing of the kind - if any body has said so, it is pure invention; these were the only spoons I had in the house, and the only ones I had bought - my wife was down at my brother's, in Hertfordshire, at the time; I have four children - two were in Clifford-street; one is concerned in a steam-packet, the other was now and then at Clifford-street - sometimes he was at Margate; I had looked at some other spoons, but not ordered them in, nor had I ordered any plate.
Q. There was a bill of sale at the time Richards took your things - you were removing from Clifford-street? A. No, I was not, but I was pressed very much by Mr. Bailey, and had immediately to raise about 700l., and I had seventeen workmen there - I ordered some plate afterwards from Mr. Hamlet: whether he was to supply me depended on the price - I had looked at a considerable quantity of second-hand plate.
Q. Did you bespeak any plate of any body else - I am not talking of looking at it? A. I think I did, of Mr. Thomas, in Bond-street; I bespoke none of any other, to my knowledge, except to Mr. Wilkinson - I believe I ordered no more; I might go into a shop, but I believe not- I am sure I did not bespeak any other plate.
Q. Did you apply for any - did you try to get any from any body else? A. No, I did not - I swear that; I do not know Mr. Oswald.
Q. In Great Russell-street - now, I tell you he is in Court? A. Oh, I will tell you; I went to look at a tea-pot there, because I did expect a sum of money - I told him to put my initials on it, but I should not take it till I got the money, and begged him to take care of it till I did - I wanted nothing but the tea-pot, and did not want to get that, for I left word I should not have it; it was taken to my house to be seen, certainly - I believe I told Mr. Oswald I wished my wife to see it; she was not living in the house then, but she would be in town in a day or two; I did not tell him I wanted a large service of plate - I said if he had got any thing cheap, I should want some - I was furnishing a house, and laid out a great deal of money; I cannot say how many persons I bought goods of on credit - I will tell you why I could not pay my way; my landlord was ill, and kept me three months without a lease, that kept me out of about 300l., which I expected, and I expected to receive some money from Manchester - other tradesmen, whom I had bought goods of, took them out of my house; no one took them by force, to my knowledge.
Q. That you will swear? A. Yes; Mr. Bailey and Mr. Wilkinson did not indict either of them for robbery- Mr. Wilkinson never applied at all, to my knowledge, for payment for his pianoforte; he, Mr. Bailey, and all came together - there were twelve or fourteen of them and their men, but I was not there; I did not take any steps - Mr. Bailey sent in a bill for 400l.; the painoforte was about 70l. I sent to Mr. Bailey, about three weeks before, that I should give him the security of a gentleman in the Lord Chamberlain's-office - I owed Mr. Plasket, the wine-merchant, 30l., Mr. Goodey, a tailor, 16l., but he owes me a little, I had some child's clothes from Mr. Lock, another tailor; I owe Mr. Whiteley, an ironmonger of Holborn, 7l.; I offered Mr. Bailey security; I told him if he was not satisfied with a gentleman he was acquainted with not to serve me, not to send any thing, and when he sent me about 20l. worth I said, "Go to the Lord Chamberlain's-office, and ask your friend again;" then he sent them - I do not know whether he took the goods away; they are not in the house now - James Rivett is the person in the Chamberlain's-office; he holds a berth there now: he gives his name on the card, and I believe he is there to-day; I do not know that he became security - I owe Mr. Foster, a linen-draper of Oxford-street, 8l. or 9l., and Mr. Blaket, of Regent-street, 8l. or 9l., Mr. Mortlock, a chinaman, of Regent-street, 40l. - I believe Mr. Williams, a silk-mercer, claims 4l.; I was to pay 160 guineas a year rent for the house in Clifford-street, besides taxes; I was to do all the repairs before I had the lease.
Q. Tell me the name of the attorney you went to after Mr. Richards robbed you? A. I mentioned it to Mr. Reynolds, of Clement-street, Kingsland-road, and to his clerk: I never saw Richard Miller, who is now a Policeman, letter C. No. 50 - I came out of the Marshalsea more than twelve months ago; I do not remember after that being in a public-house in Fleet-lane with Richard Miller and some women - I might be in Fleet lane; I remember a man, named Biggers, in Fleet-lane; I do not know a woman named Cann - I have heard of a woman named Jenkins; I was many times in Fleet-lane, next door to Biggers', at a public-house.
Q. Were you drinking in company with two women and a man there, Mrs. Jenkins being one of them? A. Very likely - I am sure I was; I have no doubt but I told a man there that I had been just discharged from the Marshalsea.
Q. The man I allude to is Richard Miller - did you ever tell him you were acting with others in getting goods and selling them? A. Never - that I am sure of; (looking at Miller) I do not recollect him - I never recollect seeing him; I might be drinking with him at a public-house in Fleet-lane - I was not a gentleman then.
Q. On your solemn oath, did you never tell a man that you were employed acting with others in getting goods and selling them, and did you not propose to Miller to join you? A. No, I did not; if I did, I must be completely in liquor, and lost - I did not propose that Mrs. Cann's husband should give the references, and that I and Mrs. Jenkins should sell the goods; I swear that positively - nor that we should share the money; it is totally untrue - nothing to that effect passed; if you could bring half a dozen to swear it, I would still deny it - if true, I would admit it.
Q. What took you to drink with a Policeman? A. If he came into the parlour to ask me a question, I do not know, he might take advantage and ask me a question, for some motive planned by other parties; it was Mrs. Rivett I took to Mr. Richards' - she is the wife of Mr. Rivett, who is in the Lord Chamberlain's office.
Q. What is he in the Lord Chamberlain's office? is not be a trumpeter? A. Yes, he is, and I will tell you what he is - he attends before His Majesty at the coronation; and he collects the Peers' fees; he lives at No. 12, Belmont-terrace; I believe I took but one lady to Richards' - the other lady whom I believed that I took, lis Mary Wright; she is a widow - I do not know where she lives; she never went to Richards' with me - I picked her up in the Borough; she sometimes came to my place in Clifford-street - I think I first met her in the London-road. in the street - I had known her a good many years in Bishopsgate-street, at No. 108 or 109; she lived with me there as servant twenty years ago.
Q. Will you swear you never went with Mary Wright to other tradesmen's houses, disguised as a lady? A. I never did - I never took her to tradesmen's houses, nor any other woman; I have a board up to sell the house in Clifford-street; I am now living in Stingo-lane, if I please - I have an objection to tell where I sleep every night; I slept in Lisson-grove last night - I slept the night before at Mountford-place, Kennington, and I slept there on Sunday, Saturday and Friday, at No. 1; my son keeps the house - my address is in Clifford-street.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320105-208]
NEW COURT. SATURDAY, JANUARY 5.
Fifth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
501. WILLIAM JUDE was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of November, 40 lbs. of potatoes, value 18d., the goods of John Walpole.
JOHN WALPOLE. I live at Enfield. On the 2nd of November I had a quantity of potatoes laying in one of my fields; I got a warrant to apprehend the prisoner, but could not get him till one day last week.
THOMAS EDWARDS. I am in the prosecutor's employ. On the 2nd of November he employed me to look after his potatoes; they were harrowed up, and were to be picked up by the women the next morning - I saw the prisoner in the field; he came out about half-past ten o'clock; a person named Walden was with him - the prisoner had a bag on his shoulder; I took Walden at the time, but the prisoner got away - there appeared to be as much in the prisoner's bag as there was in Walden's, and that had a bushel of potatoes in it; the prisoner's bag seemed to have potatoes in it - he went off as soon as he saw me run.
Prisoner. I did not run. Witness. Yes, he did, directly he saw me.
WILLIAM ARNOLD, I was in the field to keep the birds off. I saw the prisoner and Walden; the prisoner picked up potatoes, put them into a bag, and put it on his shoulder - he jumped over the hedge when he saw me coming; I told Edwards - he went after him, and the prisoner ran away.
Prisoner's Defence. I certainly was coming across the field, but it was sown with wheat - I saw a few lads picking up the green potatoes, which laid on the wheat: Mr. Walpole had cleared off all the potatoes.
MR. WALPOLE. We had harrowed the field up over night, and the potatoes were left to be cleared - there were above a ton to an acre left in the field.
THOMAS EDWARDS. There were a great many potatoes left to be picked up the next morning.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18320105-209]
502. THOMAS OWEN and HENRY POWER were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of January, 1 cloak, value 35s., the goods of Thomas Williams and George Haines.
JOHN BULL. I am shopman to Thomas Williams and George Haines silk-mercer s, of Lamb's Conduit-street. On the 5th of January, about half-past twelve o'clock, I was in the shop, and saw the prisoners lurking about the door - Owen came up on the step, pulled down a cloak, and gave it to Power, who put it into his apron, and ran away with it; I ran and caught Power with it - I took him back to the shop, then ran after Owen, and took him; I gave them both into custody - I am certain of them.
WILLIAM -. I am a Police-constable. I received the prisoners and this cloak.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Owen's Defence. I had nothing to do with the cloak, and never saw this boy till I was taken into the shop.
Power's Defence. I was going down the street, and swung my arm; the cloak being slightly hung up, came down, and this man said I took it.
MR. BULL. He had got above one hundred yards off; I had seen them some time - Owen came on the step, took it down, and gave it him.
OWEN - GUILTY. Aged 14.
POWER - GUILTY. Aged 12.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320105-210]
503. THOMAS PRITCHARD was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of January, 1 shirt, value 5s.; 1 coat, value 6s., and 15d. in copper monies, the property of Patrick Darragh.
PATRICK DARRAGH. I live in Vere-street, and am a labourer. The prisoner was apprentice to Samuel Harrison, who lives in the same house - I missed a coat on the 30th of December last, and then I missed a shirt and 1s. 3d. - the prisoner was not lodging in the house at the time, but he was taken by Baker.
JOSEPH BAKER. I am a lock-smith, and lodge where the prosecutor does. I stopped the prisoner at the corner of Vere-street on the 30th of December - his hat was taken off, and this shirt was found in it; I gave it to the officer.
SAMUEL HARRISON. The prisoner is my apprentice. -On the 30th of December he was brought into my house; I took his hat off, and found the shirt.
JOHN STEWARD. I am a Policeman. I received the prisoner and this shirt; I found 8d. in copper on him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320105-211]
504. WILLIAM PRICE was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of December, 1 shirt, value 3s., the goods of James Rogers.
The prosecutor did not appear. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320105-212]
505. SARAH YOUNG was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of January, 1 gown, value 3s., and 1 petticoat, value 2s., the goods of Catherine Ryan.
CATHERINE RYAN. I am servant to Mr. Smith, a carpenter, who lives in Sutton-street. On the afternoon of the 5th of January my master's son went out, and left the street door ajar; shortly afterwards I saw the prisoner coming down the second floor stairs - a person lodges in the second floor front room, but these articles were in the second floor back room; I saw the sleeve of my gown hanging from under her shawl; I asked her what she wanted - she said she had made a mistake, and come to the wrong house; I said she had made a grand mistake in going to my box; she attempted to pass me, and to get out, but I ran down, caught hold of her, and dragged the things from under her arm - these are them; they had been in my box on the second floor back room - the box was locked, but the key was in it; when I took the things from her, I asked her how she came by them - she said they had followed her down stairs; I am sure those were the words she used - she appeared rather intoxicated.
JOHN DAY. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoner, and have the property.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I went somewhere, but I do not know where.
GUILTY. - Aged 54. - Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18320105-213]
506. WILLIAM BRYANT was indicted for killing and slaying Elias Hart .
MARY PHILLIPS. I am the wife of Jedediah Phillips; we lived in Tooson's-court, Petticoat-lane. On the 12th of December I was coming down the court, and saw the prisoner - he made use of very bad language, and when I came back, he said, "What business have you up here?" I said, "I live up here;" he caught hold of the back of my neck, and drove me out of the court - I cried Murder! and that brought people to the spot; I got released in the crowd - I saw a large piece of wood in the prisoner's hand; I did not see that he was intoxicated.
MARK MARKS. I live in Mills-court, Petticoat-lane, and am a gold and silver refiner. On the evening of the 12th of December I heard a cry of Murder! in Tooson's-court; I went there, and saw the prisoner striking Elias Hart, whilst he laid on the ground, with part of a plumbrule - this is it; I went as soon as I heard the cry - it was between six and seven o'clock; there were some people at the bottom of the court when I ran up, but I did not see the beginning of it - the prisoner turned his back, and ran up the court; I followed him - he ran up stairs in the house where his brother, Cornelius, lives; I made several attempts to get up stairs after him, but he stood with this stick in his hand, and every time I attempted to go up, he struck it down; several persons tried to go up: he threw hot water over us, and pieces of bricks - at last a young man pulled off his coat, and ran up; he got in contact with him, and they both came down- I got hold of him by the collar, and the officer tookhim; I saw Elias Hart dead - I had known him for some years; I had not seen him give the prisoner any provocation whatever.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. A. I believe Elias Hart was a Jew, was he not? A. Yes; I ran up as soon as I heard the cry; I saw the deceased on the ground - it was light enough for me to see the prisoner strike the blows; the prisoner was not bleeding from the head at that time - I will swear there was no blood on the prisoner's face; I did not observe any person but the prisoner; I did not hear any words pass at all - Hart was an elderly man, and dealt in pawnbroker's goods - he lived up that court, I believe the prisoner is a bricklayer's labourer, and this is an instrument they use; the court is no thorougfare - I did not see any cut or bruise on the prisoner's face at all; I took him out of the passage, with the assistance of the Police-officer- I attended the inquest and was examined; I saw no blows given by the deceased - he was on the ground; I did not notice any other person about - my attention was fixed on the prisoner.
HENRY COHEN. I saw the prisoner between six and seven o'clock on the 12th of December; he had hold of Phillips by the neck, and this piece of wood in his hand -I asked what he was doing, and said, "Are you not ashamed to touch that old woman?" he let her go, and struck at me with this stick - I warded off the blow; Elias Hart then came up the court, and he struck him on the head - Hart had not done any thing to the prisoner; he was passing to his own apartment when the prisoner struck him across the head - Hart fell down, and the prisoner struck him two or three times on the temple and the head, while he was on the ground, bleeding; the prisoner then ran up stairs to his brother's room, and was taken there - Frances Phillips got a chair and sat Hart on, and washed his face; the prisoner appeared to be intoxicated.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you arrive before there was any dispufe between the prisoner and Phillips? A. I heard her call out, and then went up; I saw Hart come up, and the prisoner struck him - he had not called the prisoner, Paddy: he had not spoken to him - there was Phillips and two or three more persons, but no one called him names; I will swear Hart did not strike him or speak a word - the prisoner knocked him down without any provocation at all.
FRANCES PHILLIPS. I heard my mother cry Murder! I went to the spot - I saw the prisoner; I asked what he was doing with my mother - he made use of very had language - he left her, and poor old Mr. Hart was coming up the court - the prisoner struck at him; he had not spoken to the prisoner - he was near seventy years of age; he fell when the prisoner struck him; the prisoner repeated his blows when he was on the ground; the prisoner did not say any thing till he got to his own place; he then made use of very bad language - he did not say any thing while he was striking the deceased.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear no provocation given? A. No, I only heard my mother cry out; I did not hear such a word as Paddy; the deceased was too old to strike a blow.
THOMAS NEWMAN. I live in Tooson's-court, and am a carpenter. I heard the cry of Murder! I ran up the court, and saw Hart laying on the ground - I did not see the prisoner strike him, but I saw him in his house with his brother Cornelius - I went to fetch him down; the prisoner said, "Come up you b-rs, I want you;" he had a stick in his hand - I seized him by the leg, and brought him down; I did not observe that he was hurt in falling - I saw no blows struck, but I heard the noise of sticks going.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear no calling of names, nor provocation? A. No.
ZACHARIAH BAKER. I am a Policeman. I heard a cry of Murder! a little before seven o'clock; I got two men to assist me, and when I got to the court, there was a great multitude; when they saw us they made way for us; the witnesses and a Jewish constable were trying to get the prisoner out of the passage - I took him to the station - I went back to the house, and found this piece of a plumb-rule behind the door, covered with some staves of a washing tub; these other pieces of wood were brought in, but I do not know what they were.
JOHN CAWTHERY LODGE. I live in Swan-court, Petticoat-lane, and am a carpenter. On the night in question I heard the cry; I ran up the court, and saw the deceased laying bleeding in a corner of the court - I assisted in getting him up; a woman brought a chair - I washed his face, and took him in doors; when the officer came I took him to the station - the gentleman said, "You had better take him to a doctor," which I did - the doctor put something to his face; I took him home, and then in a coach to the London-hospital; I saw him afterwards dead.
JOHN HILLIARD. I am a pupil of the London-hospital. The deceased was brought to the hospital on the 12th of December, about ten minutes past eight o'clock; I found a deep lacerated wound on his left cheek, about two inches and a half long: the cheek bone was laid bare, and a branch of the temporal artery had been divided, which bled profusely - I should consider it was an instrument of this kind which made the wound: there was a contused wound on the bead, such as might have been inflicted by a more blunt piece of wood; several of his teeth were loose, as if he had received a blow from a man's fist - he was between sixty and seventy years of age: I stopped the haemorrhage - it had been dressed before, but not sufficiently to stop it; he died on the 2nd of January - Mr. Scott, the surgeon, superintended my management of him; he had every thing done that the nature of the ease required - had he been a young man he might have been saved; I think the wounds were the cause of his death.
Cross-examined. Q. Then you believe the cause of his death was a wound produced by an instrument of this description? A. I should think the wound on his cheek was from a blow, with the sharp edge of this piece of wood - I think it could not have been produced by falling, unless he had fallen on a scraper, or something of that kind.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that he had been attacked in Petticoat-lane by several Jews, who surrounded and robbed him; and the deceased must have fallen, in his endeavouring to get from them, but he had not struck him.
ZACHARIAH BAKER re-examined. Q. Did you see any bruise or injury on the prisoner? A. No.
JURY. Q. On what part of the cheek was the deceased's wound? A. Under the left eye; the prisoner was drunk.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Confined Twelve Months.
